=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are given sequences of positive integers A and B of length N. Process Q queries given in the following forms in the order they are given. Each query is of one of the following three types.

- 
Type 1: Given in the form 1 i x. Replace A_i with x.

- 
Type 2: Given in the form 2 i x. Replace B_i with x.

- 
Type 3: Given in the form 3 l r. Solve the following problem and print the answer.

- 
Initially, set v = 0. For i = l, l+1, ..., r in this order, replace v with either v + A_i or v \times B_i. Find the maximum possible value of v at the end.




It is guaranteed that the answers to the given type 3 queries are at most 10^{18}.

Input

The input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
N
A_1 A_2 \cdots A_N
B_1 B_2 \cdots B_N
Q
query_1
query_2
\vdots
query_Q

Here, query_i is the i-th query, given in one of the following formats:
1 i x

2 i x

3 l r

Output

Let q be the number of type 3 queries. Print q lines. The i-th line should contain the answer to the i-th type 3 query.

Constraints


- 1 \leq N \leq 10^5
- 1 \leq A_i \leq 10^9
- 1 \leq B_i \leq 10^9
- 1 \leq Q \leq 10^5
- For type 1 and 2 queries, 1 \leq i \leq N.
- For type 1 and 2 queries, 1 \leq x \leq 10^9.
- For type 3 queries, 1 \leq l \leq r \leq N.
- For type 3 queries, the value to be printed is at most 10^{18}.

Sample Input 1

3
3 2 4
1 2 2
3
3 1 3
1 1 1
3 1 3

Sample Output 1

12
7

For the first query, the answer is ((0 + A_1) \times B_2) \times B_3 = 12.
For the third query, the answer is ((0 + A_1) + A_2) + A_3 = 7.

Sample Input 2

6
65 32 12 5 8 312
4 1 3 15 16 2
6
3 2 6
3 1 5
1 5 6
2 4 9
3 2 6
3 3 5

Sample Output 2

46080
69840
27648
1728

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- spending too long comparing nearby plans before committing to one workable approach;
- returning from code to broad algorithm discussion without a concrete contradiction;
- turning one local doubt into a full restart when the current draft only needs a small patch.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Now, how to compose two IPFs: Given H (left segment) and G (right segment), compute F = G ∘ H (capped) as IPF for integer x.

If H(x)>=INF, then F(x)=INF). For y within a piece of G, G(y)=m_g*y + c_g (capped). The composition is m_g*(m_h*x+c_h)+c_g = (m_g*m_h)*x + (m_g*c_h+c_g), as long as y=H(x) lies within the G piece and y<INF (or if y>=INF, result INF). Since H is increasing, as x increases, y increases, so G pieces are traversed in order. Thus we can merge the breakpoints of H (in x) and the preimages under H of G's breakpoints (in x). If H(x)>=Y_g, then G switches to piece g. Since H is increasing, this is a single threshold.

Thus composition F's integer breakpoints are:
- The starts of H pieces (where H formula changes).
- For each G piece start Y_g (except 0), the smallest integer x >=0 such that H(x) >= Y_g. This is the preimage threshold.

Because we only evaluate at integer x, the piece of F for integer x is determined by the largest threshold <= x among H starts and G-preimage thresholds. We can merge these thresholds.

Now, how to compose two IPFs efficiently.

Let's define an IPF as list of pieces sorted by start L, with last piece m=0,c=INF (INF piece). Each piece has L, m, c. For integer x in [L, next_L), value = m*x + c (for non-final) or INF (final). We also need next_L for loops. We can store starts list and pieces list.

Given H pieces (left) and G pieces (right), compute F = G ∘ H.

Approach: Since both have small number of pieces (<=~62), we can do a two-pointer merge over "events" in x:
Events are integer x positions where either H's formula changes (H start) or G's formula changes as a function of x (preimage of G start). We can generate all candidate event positions:
- All H piece starts L_h.
- For each G piece start Y_g (including 0? The first G piece starts at 0, no event), compute X = smallest integer x >=0 such that H(x) >= Y_g. This is the x where output y reaches Y_g. Since G switches to piece starting at Y_g at that x. For Y_g=0, X=0.
Then sort unique events. For each interval between consecutive events [X_k, X_{k+1}), H is a single piece (because no H start inside) and y=H(x) lies within a single G piece (because no G threshold crossed). Then F(x) = G_piece(y) = m_g*(m_h*x+c_h)+c_g = (m_g*m_h)*x + (m_g*c_h+c_g). We can create a piece starting at X_k with this slope/intercept, capped? Since y in G piece and y < next G threshold, and if G piece is non-final, value < INF. If G piece is final INF, then m_g=0,c_g=INF, so F=INF. If H piece is final INF? H(x)=INF for x>=H_inf. Then for Y_g<=INF, X for Y_g=INF is H_inf. For Y_g<INF, H(x)>=Y_g maybe before H_inf. For x>=H_inf, H(x)=INF, so y>=INF, G final INF -> F=INF. Our event generation includes H_inf as H start (final piece). For x>=H_inf, H piece is m=0,c=INF. Then if G piece for y=INF is final INF, F=INF. Good.

Need compute X = smallest integer x with H(x) >= Y, for a given integer Y (0<=Y<=INF). H is IPF. We can find by scanning H pieces. Since H increasing. For each H piece [L, R) with formula m*x+c (or INF), find if Y is reached within this piece. Because H values at integer x. For non-final piece, m>0, c>=0. Need smallest integer x >= L such that m*x + c >= Y.
If Y <= m*L + c, then X = L (already reached at start).
Else need x >= ceil((Y - c)/m). Also x < R (next start). If x < R, then X = x. If not reached by end, continue to next piece. Since H is increasing and eventually INF, Y<=INF will be reached. For final INF piece, m=0,c=INF: if Y<=INF, reached at L (H_inf). Actually if Y=INF, X=H_inf; if Y<INF, it would have been reached earlier because H increasing and H(L)=INF >=Y, so not in final piece except maybe Y=INF. So scanning works.

But doing this for each G piece by scanning H pieces from start would be O(p*q) <=3600, still fine. But we can optimize with two-pointer. p,q<=~62, O(p*q) per merge ~4k, updates 1e5*17*4k impossible. Need O(p+q) or O(p*q) with small? 4k*1.7e6 ~6.8e9 too high. Need optimize merge to O(p+q) or at most O(p+q+events). Since p,q ~60, O(p*q) maybe 3600 per merge, too high for 1e5. Need O(p+q).

We can merge in linear time using two pointers because both H pieces and G thresholds are monotonic.

Let's design linear composition.

We have H pieces sorted by x start. G pieces sorted by y start. We want produce F pieces sorted by x start.

We can iterate over x intervals of H. For each H piece h, we know for x in [L_h, R_h), y=h(x) increases from y_left = h(L_h) to y_right = h(R_h-1) (or INF). As x increases, y traverses possibly multiple G pieces. We can advance G pointer accordingly. For each segment of x where both h and g are fixed, output a piece.

- Let h_pieces list, g_pieces list.

Need also compute H(0) and H(x) values. We can evaluate H at integer x by scanning pieces? Since x is current event, and h_idx maintained. We can compute y = h_m * x + h_c, capped to INF. If h is final, y=INF.

Let's outline compose(H, G):

Input H, G as lists of pieces (L, m, c), sorted, last INF.
Let INF = 10**18.

nH = len(H), nG = len(G)
h_idx = 0
# find g_idx for y0 = H(0)

Function eval_piece(F, x):
    if x >= INF: return INF (but x can be INF)
    pieces = F
    # find rightmost piece with L <= x
    idx = bisect_right(starts, x) - 1
    L,m,c = pieces[idx]
    if m == 0: return INF
    val = m*x + c
    if val >= INF: return INF
    return val

def make_single(A,B):
    if B == 1:
        inf_start = INF - A + 1  # since A>=1
        # If inf_start <= 0? no
        starts=[0, inf_start]
        ms=[1,0]
        cs=[A, INF]
    else:
        t = (A + B - 2)//(B - 1)  # ceil(A/(B-1))
        inf_start = (INF + B - 1)//B  # ceil(INF/B)
        starts=[0]
        ms=[1]
        cs=[A]
        if t < inf_start:
            starts.append(t)
            ms.append(B)
            cs.append(0)
        # else if t == inf_start, at x=t value INF, no multiply piece; final starts at t
        starts.append(inf_start)
        ms.append(0)
        cs.append(INF)
        # Need ensure starts sorted and no duplicates. If t == inf_start, starts [0, inf_start] (since we didn't append t? Actually if t < inf_start append t, then append inf_start. If t == inf_start, no append t, append inf_start. Good.)
        # What if t > inf_start? Shouldn't, but if B small? t<=1e9, inf_start>=1e9, so t<=inf_start. If B=1e9, inf_start=1e9, t<=1e9. If t=1e9 and inf_start=1e9, equal. If B=1e9, A=1, t=1, inf_start=1e9, t<. Good.
    return (starts, ms, cs)

Now, need build segment tree.

N up to 1e5. We can build iterative segment tree with size power of two. Each leaf stores IPF for single operation (A_i,B_i). Internal node stores compose(left, right) = F_right ∘ F_left? Need confirm order.

We process i=l..r left to right. If segment = left part then right part. Starting v, after left part value H(v), after right part G(H(v)). So node function = G ∘ H, where H=left child, G=right child. In compose(H,G) we defined F=G∘H. So internal = compose(left, right). Good.

Query [l,r): We need apply functions in order. Iterative segment tree query typically collects left nodes and right nodes. We can maintain v and apply selected node functions as we traverse in order. Simpler: use recursive query? Or iterative with two accumulators? Since composition order matters. We can implement query by decomposing into a list of nodes in left-to-right order, then apply each function to v. Number of nodes O(log N). Collecting list and applying is fine. Or use iterative with left_stack/right_stack.

Simplest:
def range_query(l,r): # 0-index [l,r)
    l += size; r += size
    left_nodes = []
    right_nodes = []
    while l < r:
        if l & 1:
            left_nodes.append(l); l += 1
        if r & 1:
            r -= 1; right_nodes.append(r)
        l >>= 1; r >>= 1
    nodes = left_nodes + right_nodes[::-1]
    v = 0
    for node in nodes:
        v = eval_func(tree[node], v)
    return v

This applies functions in correct order. O(log N) nodes, eval O(log pieces).

Type2 similar.

Now, let's think about correctness of IPF representation more formally.

We represent F_cap(x) = min(F(x), INF) for integer x. Each operation T_i is max of two affine functions. Composition is max of affine functions, hence convex piecewise-linear. The integer-snapped function is piecewise linear with integer breakpoints. Our compose computes exact integer-snapped function of composition of capped functions. Need ensure capping at INF doesn't affect values <INF. Since all operations are monotone and F(x)>=x, cap(F(G(x))) = cap(F(cap(G(x)))) for integer x? If G(x)>INF, then F(G(x))>=G(x)>INF, so cap=INF. cap(G)=INF, F(INF)>=INF, cap=INF. If G(x)<=INF, cap(G)=G. So yes.

Now, single operation IPF exact:
For B>1, T(x)=max(x+A, Bx). For integer x:
- If x < t=ceil(A/(B-1)), then x(B-1) < A? Let's check if x < ceil(A/(B-1)), then x <= ceil(...)-1. Need ensure x(B-1) < A. Since x < A/(B-1) iff x(B-1)<A. Ceil condition: x < ceil(A/(B-1)) implies x < A/(B-1)? If A/(B-1) integer, ceil=value, x<value => x < rational. If non-integer, ceil=floor+1, x<=floor < rational. So yes. Thus add > multiply? At x < t, x+A > Bx? Difference A - x(B-1) >0. So T=x+A.
- If x >= t, x(B-1) >= A? If A/(B-1) integer and x>=value, yes. If non-integer, x>=ceil > rational, yes. So multiply >= add. T=Bx.
Capped at INF: add piece x+A < INF because x<t<=1e9, A<=1e9, sum<=2e9<INF. Multiply piece Bx reaches INF at ceil(INF/B). If inf_start == t, then at x=t, Bx>=INF, so T=INF; no non-final multiply piece. Our make_single handles by only adding multiply piece if t < inf_start. But what if t < inf_start and B*t could be >INF? Since t < inf_start = ceil(INF/B) implies t < INF/B, so B*t < INF. Good. If t == inf_start, B*t >= INF, so T=INF at t. Add piece for x<t. Good.
For B=1, T=x+A, reaches INF at INF-A+1. If A=INF? A<=1e9, no.

Now, compose exactness with integer snapping:
Given H and G IPFs representing capped functions for integer x. For each integer x, H(x) integer. G is represented by pieces over integer y. The composition F(x)=G(H(x)). Our merge partitions integer x into intervals where H is a single affine piece and H(x) lies in a single G piece. Since H is increasing, the set of x where H(x) < next G start is an interval; the boundary is the smallest integer x with H(x) >= Y. We compute that. Thus exact.

Need consider H(x) may not be integer? H is composition of operations with integer coefficients and integer x, so integer. Good.

Now, performance of compose with while loop and eval at top. We need avoid computing y from scratch each event maybe O(1) using h_idx. We'll compute y = mh[h_idx]*x + ch[h_idx]; if y>=INF cap. Since m capped, product small (<=1e18+1e9). Good.

But note: mh[h_idx] may be INF+1, x up to INF, product ~1e36 if not capped? Wait we cap m to INF+1, so product (INF+1)*INF ~1e36, Python int okay but large. But if h_idx piece is non-final, can x be large with m=INF+1? We argued no, but our code might still compute y for x at event before detecting final? Suppose h piece [0,1) with m=INF+1, and x becomes 1. At x=1, h_idx should have advanced to next piece (final INF) because H piece end at 1. If we don't advance? We do. So we won't compute m=INF+1 at x=1. But what if h piece [0, INF+1?) no. So product not too large. However, in top before advancing h at x=1? We advance at end of previous iteration when x=1. So h_idx updated. Good. But if next_x = X = 1 and h end also 1, we advance. So safe. Still, Python big ints up to 1e36 are fine.

Need ensure starts list sorted and no duplicate. In compose, when adding piece at x, if x equals previous start? We only add when next_x > x, so x increases. But if we break and add final INF at x, x may equal previous start? No, previous piece ended at x-1, so start x > previous start. If previous piece was final? We break before adding non-final after final? We break when final, so no.

Now, normalize after compose:
We can integrate merging adjacent same m,c in add_piece. But if we break and add final INF, and previous piece already m=0,c=INF? We check.

Need also remove duplicate starts if two events at same x cause adding zero-length? We don't add zero-length. But after advancing h at x, top may add a piece at same x. That's fine: previous piece ended at x-1, new piece starts at x. If previous piece was added with same x? No.

Let's write compose carefully with local variables for speed.

Pseudo code:

def compose(H, G):
    sh, mh, ch = H
    sg, mg, cg = G
    nh = len(sh); ng = len(sg)
    h_idx = 0
    # initial y0
    if mh[0] == 0:
        y0 = INF
    else:
        y0 = mh[0]*0 + ch[0]  # ch[0]
        if y0 >= INF: y0 = INF
    g_idx = 0
    while g_idx + 1 < ng and sg[g_idx+1] <= y0:
        g_idx += 1

    res_starts=[]; res_ms=[]; res_cs=[]
    x = 0
    while x <= INF:
        # advance h_idx? Should be correct. But if x == sh[h_idx]? h_idx points to piece with start <=x. Initially 0. After advancing at end, okay.
        # compute y
        hm = mh[h_idx]
        if hm == 0:
            y = INF
        else:
            y = hm * x + ch[h_idx]
            if y >= INF:
                y = INF
        # advance g
        while g_idx + 1 < ng and sg[g_idx+1] <= y:
            g_idx += 1

        # if final
        if g_idx == ng - 1 or hm == 0:
            # add final INF at x if not already
            if not (res_ms and res_ms[-1] == 0 and res_cs[-1] == INF):
                # But if previous piece already final with start < x, we shouldn't add duplicate; if previous final, just break.
                if res_ms and res_ms[-1] == 0 and res_cs[-1] == INF:
                    break
                res_starts.append(x); res_ms.append(0); res_cs.append(INF)
            break

        # next_x
        next_x = INF + 1
        if h_idx + 1 < nh:
            R = sh[h_idx+1]
            if R < next_x: next_x = R
        # next G threshold
        Y = sg[g_idx+1]
        # current y < Y
        num = Y - ch[h_idx]
        # num should >0
        if num <= 0:
            X = x
        else:
            X = (num + hm - 1)//hm
        if X < next_x:
            next_x = X
        # If next_x <= x? Shouldn't. But if X == x? num>0 and hm>0 => X > x. If num<=0, X=x but y>=Y contradiction. So next_x > x.
        if next_x <= x:
            # fallback: advance g? shouldn't
            next_x = x + 1

        # create piece [x, next_x)
        m = mg[g_idx] * hm
        c = mg[g_idx] * ch[h_idx] + cg[g_idx]
        if m > INF + 1:
            m = INF + 1
        # Check if value at x >= INF
        if m != 0 and m * x + c >= INF:
            # final INF at x
            if not (res_ms and res_ms[-1] == 0 and res_cs[-1] == INF):
                res_starts.append(x); res_ms.append(0); res_cs.append(INF)
            break
        # add non-final piece
        if res_ms and res_ms[-1] == m and res_cs[-1] == c:
            pass
        else:
            res_starts.append(x); res_ms.append(m); res_cs.append(c)

        x = next_x
        if x > INF:
            break
        if h_idx + 1 < nh and sh[h_idx+1] == x:
            h_idx += 1

    # Ensure final INF piece exists
    if not (res_ms and res_ms[-1] == 0 and res_cs[-1] == INF):
        # If res empty? Shouldn't. Add at x? x may be INF+1. Use INF.
        start = x if x <= INF else INF
        # If previous piece covers up to INF and is non-final, final starts at INF.

def eval_func(F, x):
    if x >= INF:
        return INF
    starts, ms, cs = F
    idx = bisect_right(starts, x) - 1
    m = ms[idx]
    if m == 0:
        return INF
    val = m*x + cs[idx]
    if val >= INF:
        return INF
    return val

Let's design flat IPF functions.

Representation: F is a tuple of ints: (L0, m0, c0, L1, m1, c1, ..., L_{k-1}, m_{k-1}, c_{k-1})
Last piece m=0,c=INF.

Helper:
def piece_count(F): return len(F)//3
def get_piece(F, idx): return F[3*idx], F[3*idx+1], F[3*idx+2]

Eval:
def eval_func(F, x):
    if x >= INF:
        return INF
    # find last piece with L <= x
    # linear scan from end? Since k small, but query many. Use binary search on L.
    lo=0; hi=len(F)//3 - 1
    # find rightmost L <= x
    # Since L sorted.
    while lo < hi:
        mid = (lo+hi+1)//2
        if F[3*mid] <= x:
            lo = mid
        else:
            hi = mid - 1
    idx = lo
    m = F[3*idx+1]
    if m == 0:
        return INF
    val = m*x + F[3*idx+2]
    if val >= INF:
        return INF
    return val

Binary search on flat tuple with stride 3. Good.

def compose(H, G):
    sh = H[0::3]? Slicing creates lists, not good. We can access via indices.
    nh = len(H)//3; ng = len(G)//3
    h_idx=0
    # y0 = H(0): first piece c = H[2] if m!=0 else INF
    if H[1] == 0: y0=INF
    else: y0=H[2]; if y0>=INF: y0=INF
    g_idx=0
    while g_idx+1 < ng and G[3*(g_idx+1)] <= y0:
        g_idx += 1
    res=[]  # list of ints
    x=0
    while x <= INF:
        hm = H[3*h_idx+1]
        if hm == 0:
            y = INF
        else:
            y = hm * x + H[3*h_idx+2]
            if y >= INF: y = INF
        while g_idx+1 < ng and G[3*(g_idx+1)] <= y:
            g_idx += 1
        if g_idx == ng-1 or hm == 0:
            # add final INF at x if not already
            if not (res and res[-2] == 0 and res[-1] == INF):  # res[-2] is m, res[-1] c? Wait flat triple: L,m,c. If last piece final, res[-3]=L, res[-2]=m, res[-1]=c.
                # check if last piece is final
                if len(res) >= 3 and res[-2] == 0 and res[-1] == INF:
                    break
                res.extend([x, 0, INF])
            break
        next_x = INF + 1
        if h_idx + 1 < nh:
            R = H[3*(h_idx+1)]
            if R < next_x: next_x = R
        Y = G[3*(g_idx+1)]
        hc = H[3*h_idx+2]
        num = Y - hc
        if num <= 0:
            X = x
        else:
            X = (num + hm - 1)//hm
        if X < next_x:
            next_x = X
        if next_x <= x:
            next_x = x + 1  # safety
        gm = G[3*g_idx+1]
        gc = G[3*g_idx+2]
        m = gm * hm
        c = gm * hc + gc
        if m > INF + 1:
            m = INF + 1
        if m != 0 and m * x + c >= INF:
            if not (len(res) >= 3 and res[-2] == 0 and res[-1] == INF):
                res.extend([x, 0, INF])
            break
        # add non-final piece if not same as previous
        if len(res) >= 3 and res[-2] == m and res[-1] == c:
            pass
        else:
            res.extend([x, m, c])
        x = next_x
        if x > INF:
            break
        if h_idx + 1 < nh and H[3*(h_idx+1)] == x:
            h_idx += 1
    # ensure final INF
    if not (len(res) >= 3 and res[-2] == 0 and res[-1] == INF):
        start = x if x <= INF else INF
        res.extend([start, 0, INF])
    return tuple(res)

def make_single(A,B):
    if B == 1:
        inf_start = INF - A + 1
        return (0, 1, A, inf_start, 0, INF)
    else:
        t = (A + B - 2)//(B - 1)
        inf_start = (INF + B - 1)//B
        if t < inf_start:
            return (0, 1, A, t, B, 0, inf_start, 0, INF)
        else:
            return (0, 1, A, inf_start, 0, INF)

import sys, bisect
INF = 10**18
CAP_M = INF + 1

def make_single(A,B): ...
def compose(H,G): ...
def eval_func(F,x): ...
def main():
    input = sys.stdin.buffer.readline
    N = int(input())
    A = list(map(int, input().split()))
    B = list(map(int, input().split()))
    Q = int(input())
    size=1
    while size<N: size <<=1
    ID = (0,1,0,INF,0,INF)
    tree=[ID]*(2*size)
    for i in range(N):
        tree[size+i]=make_single(A[i],B[i])
    for i in range(N,size):
        tree[size+i]=ID
    for p in range(size-1,0,-1):
        tree[p]=compose(tree[2*p], tree[2*p+1])
    out=[]
    for _ in range(Q):
        query = list(map(int, input().split()))
        if query[0]==1:
            _,i,x = query
            i-=1
            A[i]=x
            tree[size+i]=make_single(A[i], B[i])
            p=(size+i)//2
            while p:
                tree[p]=compose(tree[2*p], tree[2*p+1])
                p//=2
        elif query[0]==2:
            _,i,x = query
            i-=1
            B[i]=x
            tree[size+i]=make_single(A[i], B[i])
            p=(size+i)//2
            while p:
                tree[p]=compose(tree[2*p], tree[2*p+1])
                p//=2
        else:
            _,l,r = query
            l-=1; r-=1 # inclusive? Input 1-index inclusive. Convert to 0-index half-open [l-1, r)
            l += 0? Let's do l -=1; r = r  # because r inclusive, half-open end = r (since 1-index r -> 0-index r-1 inclusive, end = r)
            # Example l=1,r=3 -> 0-index [0,3). l=0, r=3. So l-=1, r unchanged.
            l0 = l-1? Wait:
            If input l=1,r=3, want indices 0,1,2. Half-open [0,3). So l = l-1 =0, r = r =3. So:
            l -= 1
            # r remains r
            left_nodes=[]; right_nodes=[]
            l += size; r += size
            while l<r:
                if l&1:
                    left_nodes.append(l); l+=1
                if r&1:
                    r-=1; right_nodes.append(r)
                l>>=1; r>>=1
            nodes = left_nodes + right_nodes[::-1]
            v=0
            for node in nodes:
                v = eval_func(tree[node], v)
            out.append(str(v))
    sys.stdout.write("\n".join(out))

Let's rewrite compose with current piece variables.

def compose(H,G):
    nh = len(H)//3; ng = len(G)//3
    h_idx=0; g_idx=0
    # initial g_idx
    hm0 = H[1]
    if hm0 == 0:
        y0 = INF
    else:
        y0 = H[2]
        if y0 >= INF: y0 = INF
    while g_idx+1 < ng and G[3*(g_idx+1)] <= y0:
        g_idx += 1
    hL = H[0]; hm = H[1]; hc = H[2]
    gL = G[3*g_idx]; gm = G[3*g_idx+1]; gc = G[3*g_idx+2]
    res=[]
    x=0
    while x <= INF:
        # compute y
        if hm == 0:
            y = INF
        else:
            y = hm * x + hc
            if y >= INF:
                y = INF
        # advance g
        while g_idx+1 < ng and G[3*(g_idx+1)] <= y:
            g_idx += 1
            gL = G[3*g_idx]; gm = G[3*g_idx+1]; gc = G[3*g_idx+2]
        if g_idx == ng-1 or hm == 0:
            if len(res)>=3 and res[-2]==0 and res[-1]==INF:
                break
            res.extend((x,0,INF))
            break
        next_x = INF + 1
        if h_idx+1 < nh:
            R = H[3*(h_idx+1)]
            if R < next_x: next_x = R
        Y = G[3*(g_idx+1)]
        num = Y - hc
        if num <= 0:
            X = x
        else:
            X = (num + hm - 1)//hm
        if X < next_x:
            next_x = X
        if next_x <= x:
            next_x = x + 1
        m = gm * hm
        c = gm * hc + gc
        if m > CAP:
            m = CAP
        if m != 0 and m * x + c >= INF:
            if len(res)>=3 and res[-2]==0 and res[-1]==INF:
                break
            res.extend((x,0,INF))
            break
        if len(res)>=3 and res[-2]==m and res[-1]==c:
            pass
        else:
            res.extend((x,m,c))
        x = next_x
        if x > INF:
            break
        if h_idx+1 < nh and H[3*(h_idx+1)] == x:
            h_idx += 1
            hL = H[3*h_idx]; hm = H[3*h_idx+1]; hc = H[3*h_idx+2]
    if not (len(res)>=3 and res[-2]==0 and res[-1]==INF):
        start = x if x <= INF else INF
        res.extend((start,0,INF))
    return tuple(res)

Now, eval_func with flat tuple and binary search. Since k small, linear scan might be faster than binary search due to overhead? Query nodes ~17, eval 1e5 ->1.7M eval. Each eval binary search ~6 iterations, linear scan average 30. Binary search better. Implement with while.

def eval_func(F,x):
    if x >= INF:
        return INF
    lo=0; hi=len(F)//3 - 1
    while lo < hi:
        mid = (lo+hi+1)//2
        if F[3*mid] <= x:
            lo = mid
        else:
            hi = mid - 1
    m = F[3*lo+1]
    if m == 0:
        return INF
    val = m*x + F[3*lo+2]
    if val >= INF:
        return INF
    return val

Since F[3*mid] indexing. Good.

Now, let's attempt to construct a worst-case for piece count and compose. Suppose all B=2, A_i=1. We computed F for n=3 had 2 pieces. Not worst. To maximize pieces, need A_i increasing exponentially. Let's analyze.

For a positive breakpoint at operation i, need S_{i-1} < τ_i = A_i/(B_i-1). Since A_i<=1e9 and B_i>=2, τ_i <= A_i <=1e9 (if B_i large, τ_i smaller). Thus S_{i-1} < 1e9. Then S_i = S_{i-1}+A_i > B_i S_{i-1} >=2 S_{i-1}. So S doubles while S<1e9. Starting S>=1, number of doublings <= log2(1e9) ~30. After S>=1e9, no operation can have a positive breakpoint because τ_i <=1e9 <= S_{i-1}. Thus total positive breakpoints <=30! This is even better. Wait what if B_i=1? No breakpoint. If B_i>1, τ_i = A_i/(B_i-1) <= A_i <=1e9. So yes. Therefore piece count <= ~31 plus INF piece. Great. This means compose loops <=~32. Very fast.

But is S_i = F(0) for prefix? If operation already multiplying at x=0, S_i = S_{i-1}*B_i, which can exceed 1e9 without a breakpoint. But such operations don't add pieces. Positive breakpoints only when S_{i-1} < τ_i <=1e9. So count <=30. Nice. This also means piece count bound independent of INF. Good.

Wait, what about a segment not starting at 0? F(0) for that segment is sum of A in segment if no multiplications? Actually F(0) can be >1e9 due to multiplications even if segment starts at 0. But positive breakpoints require S_{i-1} < τ_i <=1e9. So before each breakpoint, S<1e9. After breakpoint, S doubles. So count <=30. Good. If F(0)>1e9, no more breakpoints. Thus piece count <=31. Excellent.

Let's verify with A_i<=1e9, B_i<=1e9. τ_i <= A_i/(B_i-1) <= A_i <=1e9. If B_i=2, τ=A_i. Need S < A_i <=1e9. So yes.

Thus each IPF has at most ~32 pieces (including final INF). This makes compose very fast and memory low.

Before final, let's maybe try to prove the piece count bound with A_i<=1e9 for our own confidence:
For any segment function F, consider building it by composing operations. A breakpoint in F occurs when some operation i changes from add to multiply at some input value. For that to happen, the value before operation i at x=0 must be < τ_i = A_i/(B_i-1) <= A_i <=1e9. If it is a breakpoint, then at x=0 operation i chooses add, so S_i = S_{i-1}+A_i. Also S_{i-1} < A_i/(B_i-1), so A_i > (B_i-1)S_{i-1}, hence S_i > B_i S_{i-1} >=2 S_{i-1}. Thus each breakpoint at least doubles S, while S<1e9 before it. Starting S_0=0, first breakpoint S_1=A1<=1e9. Number of doublings from 1 to 1e9 is about 30. More formally, if there are k breakpoints, S_k >= 2^{k-1} (for k>=1) and S_{k-1}<1e9, so k <= 31. Thus piece count <=31 + final INF. Good. This is a nice property; not needed in code.

Now, let's think about if a breakpoint can occur when S_{i-1}=0 for first operation. Then S_i=A1<=1e9. Count 1. Subsequent breakpoints double. So max pieces ~31. Good.

Now, let's consider if a segment not starting at 0 has F(0) maybe not equal to sum of A? It is the max value starting from 0 within that segment. The same argument applies to the sequence of operations in that segment. So yes.

Now, let's think about if operations with B_i=1 can increase S without doubling and allow more breakpoints? B_i=1 operations have no breakpoint, so they don't count. They can increase S, making it harder for future breakpoints (since S must be <τ<=1e9). So they don't increase count. Good.

Now, let's consider if B_i>1 but τ_i could be >1e9? τ=A/(B-1) <= A <=1e9, so no.

Thus piece count small.

Now, let's think about if there is any hidden pitfall: The operation is "replace v with either v + A_i or v × B_i". We assume we can choose per i to maximize final. Since the choice at each step can depend on current v, and we want maximum final, DP with max is correct. There is no constraint that choices must be made offline; max at each step is optimal due to monotonicity? Actually dynamic programming: dp_i(v)=max(dp_{i+1}(v+A_i), dp_{i+1}(v*B_i)). Since dp_{i+1} is increasing, the optimal choice at step i for a given v is max(v+A_i, v*B_i) only if dp_{i+1} is increasing and we can choose based on v. But dp_{i+1} is increasing, so maximizing the immediate value also maximizes final? Need check: If dp_{i+1}(y) is increasing in y, then for a given v, the best is to choose the operation that yields larger y. Yes. Since all future operations are monotone, larger current v is always better. Thus T_i(v)=max(v+A_i, v*B_i). Good.

import sys
from bisect import bisect_right? We use custom binary search, no import.

INF = 10**18
CAP = INF + 1

def make_single(A, B, INF=INF):
    if B == 1:
        inf_start = INF - A
        return (0, 1, A, inf_start, 0, INF)
    t = (A + B - 2) // (B - 1)
    inf_start = (INF + B - 1) // B
    if t < inf_start:
        return (0, 1, A, t, B, 0, inf_start, 0, INF)
    else:
        return (0, 1, A, inf_start, 0, INF)

Wait if t < inf_start, we append t,B,0 then inf_start,0,INF. If t == inf_start, no multiply. If t > inf_start, no multiply. Good.

But what if t < inf_start and B*t == INF? impossible because t < ceil(INF/B) => t <= ceil-1 < INF/B, so B*t < INF. Good.

Now, compose:

def compose(H, G, INF=INF, CAP=CAP):
    nh = len(H)//3
    ng = len(G)//3
    h_idx = 0
    # initial g_idx
    if H[1] == 0:
        y0 = INF
    else:
        y0 = H[2]
        if y0 >= INF:
            y0 = INF
    g_idx = 0
    while g_idx + 1 < ng and G[3*(g_idx+1)] <= y0:
        g_idx += 1

    hm = H[1]
    hc = H[2]
    gm = G[3*g_idx+1]
    gc = G[3*g_idx+2]
    res = []
    x = 0
    while x <= INF:
        if hm == 0:
            y = INF
        else:
            y = hm * x + hc
            if y >= INF:
                y = INF
        while g_idx + 1 < ng and G[3*(g_idx+1)] <= y:
            g_idx += 1
            gm = G[3*g_idx+1]
            gc = G[3*g_idx+2]
        if g_idx == ng - 1 or hm == 0:
            if len(res) >= 3 and res[-2] == 0 and res[-1] == INF:
                break
            res.extend((x, 0, INF))
            break
        next_x = INF + 1
        if h_idx + 1 < nh:
            R = H[3*(h_idx+1)]
            if R < next_x:
                next_x = R
        Y = G[3*(g_idx+1)]
        num = Y - hc
        if num <= 0:
            X = x
        else:
            X = (num + hm - 1) // hm
        if X < next_x:
            next_x = X
        if next_x <= x:
            next_x = x + 1
        m = gm * hm
        c = gm * hc + gc
        if m > CAP:
            m = CAP
        if m != 0 and m * x + c >= INF:
            if len(res) >= 3 and res[-2] == 0 and res[-1] == INF:
                break
            res.extend((x, 0, INF))
            break
        if len(res) >= 3 and res[-2] == m and res[-1] == c:
            pass
        else:
            res.extend((x, m, c))
        x = next_x
        if x > INF:
            break
        if h_idx + 1 < nh and H[3*(h_idx+1)] == x:
            h_idx += 1
            hm = H[3*h_idx+1]
            hc = H[3*h_idx+2]
    if not (len(res) >= 3 and res[-2] == 0 and res[-1] == INF):
        start = x if x <= INF else INF
        res.extend((start, 0, INF))
    return tuple(res)

Potential bug: In initial g_idx, if H[1]==0, y0=INF. But if H first piece final INF, H[2] is INF. Good. If H[1]!=0, y0=H[2] (since x=0). If H[2] could be >=INF? Then y0=INF. Good.

Potential bug: When h_idx advances, we update hm,hc but not if h_idx becomes final? hm=0. Good.

Potential bug: If h_idx advances and H[3*h_idx+1] maybe 0, hm=0. Good.

Potential bug: If g_idx advances, we update gm,gc but if g_idx becomes final, gm=0,gc=INF. Good.

Potential bug: In the final block, if res already final, break. But if res already final and x maybe less than res final start? Not possible. If res final, we would have broken immediately after adding, so no further loop. But safe.

Potential bug: In the m*x+c>=INF block, if res already final, break. But if res final, we would have broken earlier. Safe.

Potential bug: If next_x = x+1 due to safety, and x=INF, next_x=INF+1, add piece maybe? But final block should catch. Good.

Now, eval_func:

def eval_func(F, x, INF=INF):
    if x >= INF:
        return INF
    lo = 0
    hi = len(F)//3 - 1
    while lo < hi:
        mid = (lo + hi + 1)//2
        if F[3*mid] <= x:
            lo = mid
        else:
            hi = mid - 1
    m = F[3*lo+1]
    if m == 0:
        return INF
    val = m * x + F[3*lo+2]
    if val >= INF:
        return INF
    return val

Now, main:

def main():
    input = sys.stdin.buffer.readline
    N = int(input())
    A = list(map(int, input().split()))
    B = list(map(int, input().split()))
    Q = int(input())
    size = 1
    while size < N:
        size <<= 1
    ID = (0, 1, 0, INF, 0, INF)
    tree = [ID] * (2 * size)
    for i in range(N):
        tree[size + i] = make_single(A[i], B[i])
    for p in range(size - 1, 0, -1):
        tree[p] = compose(tree[2*p], tree[2*p+1])
    out = []
    for _ in range(Q):
        q = list(map(int, input().split()))
        if q[0] == 1:
            _, i, x = q
            i -= 1
            A[i] = x
            tree[size + i] = make_single(A[i], B[i])
            p = (size + i) // 2
            while p:
                tree[p] = compose(tree[2*p], tree[2*p+1])
                p //= 2
        elif q[0] == 2:
            _, i, x = q
            i -= 1
            B[i] = x
            tree[size + i] = make_single(A[i], B[i])
            p = (size + i) // 2
            while p:
                tree[p] = compose(tree[2*p], tree[2*p+1])
                p //= 2
        else:
            _, l, r = q
            l -= 1
            # r is already exclusive after 1-index? Let's verify:
            # input l,r 1-index inclusive. 0-index inclusive [l-1, r-1]. Half-open end = r.
            # So l = l-1, r remains r.
            l += size
            r += size
            left_nodes = []
            right_nodes = []
            while l < r:
                if l & 1:
                    left_nodes.append(l)
                    l += 1
                if r & 1:
                    r -= 1
                    right_nodes.append(r)
                l >>= 1
                r >>= 1
            v = 0
            for node in left_nodes:
                v = eval_func(tree[node], v)
            for node in reversed(right_nodes):
                v = eval_func(tree[node], v)
            out.append(str(v))
    sys.stdout.write("\n".join(out))

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

Leaves:
leaf0 B=1 A=3: (0,1,3, INF-3,0,INF)

node2=compose(leaf0, leaf1) = F1∘F0.
Let's compute roughly:
H=leaf0 (x+3), G=leaf1.
y0=3, G next start 2 <=3, so g_idx advances to multiply piece [2,2,0]. So G uses 2y for y>=2.
H piece [0,INF-3): y=x+3. G next Y=5e17. X=ceil((5e17-0)/2?) Wait G multiply piece m=2,c=0, next start inf=5e17. H y=x+3. Need H(x)>=5e17 => x>=5e17-3. H end INF-3=1e18-3. next_x=5e17-3. Output piece m=2*1=2,c=2*3+0=6 -> F=2x+6 for x<5e17-3. At x=0, 6. Actual after leaf0 v=3, leaf1 max(5,6)=6. Good. Then final at 5e17-3. Node2 first piece slope2.
node3=compose(leaf2, ID)=leaf2.
root=compose(node2, leaf2) = F2∘node2.
Query v=0: eval node2 at0 =6. eval leaf2 at6: leaf2 add piece for x<4? Actually leaf2 F2(y)=y+4 for y<4, 2y for y>=4. 6>=4, multiply 12. Output12. Good.

After update leaf0 A=1: leaf0 x+1. node2: y0=1, G next start2 >1, g_idx=0 (add piece). H y=x+1, G add piece y+2 for y<2, multiply for y>=2. H crosses 2 at x=1. Output piece [0,1): m=1,c=1+2=3 -> x+3. At x=0,3? Wait after leaf0 v=1, leaf1 max(3,2)=3. Yes. Then at x=1, y=2, G multiply. Root eval at0: node2=3, leaf2 at3: add? leaf2 threshold4, 3<4, add 7. Output7. Good.
